G234 De-duplication by "has a controller" quietly deleted the workloads that matter most on a live cluster. The attack-path scan skips controller-owned workloads so that a Deployment, its ReplicaSet and its Pods count once instead of three times. "Has a controller" is the wrong test for that. Everything a workload operator creates is controller-owned by its custom resource, so on a live KServe cluster the Deployment behind the predictor -- the one actually running the privileged container, selected by a LoadBalancer Service -- was dropped, and the scan reported nothing. --live is exactly what the scan's own limitation text tells a reader to try. Two conditions now, not one: the controller must be a kind whose own pod template carries the same spec (an ordinary workload kind, never a CRD whose spec this scan may not read at all), and that owner must actually be in the input. The second condition is the one that is easy to miss: dropping on the owner's kind alone would lose every ReplicaSet in a namespace scanned without its Deployments -- the same silent miss moved one object sideways. The cost of being wrong in the safe direction is one duplicated path; the cost of being wrong in the other is an internet-facing privileged pod that no report mentions. The owner index is built once per scan, because the first version built it per object and VUL1's complexity guard failed in the same run -- a correctness fix that makes the scan quadratic is not a fix tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_workload_owned_by_a_crd_is_still_scored, tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_deployment_and_its_own_replica_set_are_still_one_workload, tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_an_orphan_replica_set_is_scored_rather_than_dropped, tests/test_vuln_redteam.py::test_reach_looks_up_the_inventory_a_fixed_number_of_times
G235 The product knew the answer and gave evidence-gathering steps instead. insight is the front door: paste anything and it recognises what it is. Asked a question in WORDS about a job this product does, it ran the generic gather steps -- get pods, describe pod, get events, logs --previous -- and returned nothing. Measured on eighteen questions a platform engineer would actually type, eight came back with no findings and no next step at all, and "which of our exposed services could reach a node?" got pod-triage commands: steps that answer "something is broken, what is it", which is a different question from the one asked, while posture scan answers exactly that one and was never mentioned. For a tool whose adoption argument is "paste anything", a confident irrelevant answer is the worst available outcome. CAPABILITY_ROUTES maps the question to the command: eighteen routes, keyword-matched and deterministic -- no AI inside, which is the positioning -- surfaced as answeredBy beside the cluster steps rather than mixed into them, because those are commands against a cluster and these are commands against this product. Sixteen of the eighteen questions now reach the right command first; the other two already answered themselves with findings. The work was almost entirely in NOT over-matching, and every tightening came from a measured false positive: gate answered a Helm render error because "fail" was in both halves of its pattern; posture scan answered "can a pod in shop reach a pod in payments" because "pods" counted as a target, when that is a NetworkPolicy question; and the build analyser answered an ImagePullBackOff because "image" plus "failed" matched, when a runtime pull failure is not a build failure. Each needs two of the asking words, not one; sixteen pasted errors and a multi-line manifest stay silent; a question gets two suggestions at most, not a menu. The audit route's text states no rule count, because a number typed into a sentence is the G212 defect with a new hiding place. Two claims in the first version of this row were false and are corrected by G237: that every command named was checked against the CLI's parser (the check passed the longest real PREFIX, so vuln rank passed on vuln and eight of twenty printed invocations could not be run), and that pasted input stayed silent (nothing consulted what had been recognised, so a pasted Event whose pod was called gate-7d9f was answered with the CI gate) tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_question_reaches_the_command_that_answers_it, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_every_routed_command_actually_parses, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_pasted_error_is_not_answered_with_a_command_for_something_else, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_pasted_manifest_or_log_is_never_routed, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_at_most_two_suggestions, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_the_insight_response_carries_the_routes_and_says_so, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_the_terminal_prints_them
G236 /metrics, and the four decisions that make a metrics endpoint safe rather than merely present. A platform team will not run a pod it cannot alert on, and until this the server had no /metrics, the chart had no PrometheusRule or ServiceMonitor, and the incident watch could parse an Alertmanager payload with nowhere for a receiver to post one -- a capability that existed and was unreachable. N11, and platform requirement R6.5's missing third. Instrumented at run_tool, the single point both the MCP tools and the REST routes pass through, so the two surfaces cannot report differently; no new dependency, because the exposition format is four fields per line and a hash-pinned library for that is a poor trade in a tool whose argument is that it runs disconnected. The decisions are the content. Bounded labels: run_tool labels an anticipated failure with the exception's class name, and exception types are open-ended, so a caller able to provoke a new one would add a Prometheus label value every time -- a cardinality attack on the monitoring system rather than on this process. Every label value is mapped into a fixed set first and tool is accepted only when the toolkit actually serves it. Nothing about the caller: tool, surface, outcome, duration. A scrape is readable by anyone who can reach the port and is kept for months, which makes it the last place customer data should appear, and a test asserts the label key set rather than trusting the review. Guarded by wrapper order: /metrics sits inside BearerAuth unless server.metrics.allowUnauthenticated says otherwise, because the document names every tool a deployment serves and how often each is called, which is operational intelligence about the team running it; opening the scrape must not open the tools, and a test checks that too. Two dates, not one: knowledge age is the IMAGE's review date, fixed at build time, and the corpus date exists only where a corpus is imported -- so they are separate metrics, the corpus one is absent rather than zero when there is none (a zero reads as 1970), and the alert on the first says in its own description that it is about the image and not a failed refresh. Two chart rules follow the same reasoning: both objects are off by default, because rendering a CRD the cluster does not serve fails the install, and enabling the PrometheusRule without the ServiceMonitor is refused -- an alert on a metric nobody scrapes never fires, and silence is indistinguishable from health. The error-ratio alert counts crash and error and deliberately not invalid-arguments, because a caller sending bad input is not this pod failing and paging someone for somebody else's typo teaches them to mute the alert. One guard is worth more than the rest: every kube_noob_* metric named anywhere in the PrometheusRule is checked against what the renderer actually emits, so an alert can never be written against a metric that does not exist tests/test_metrics.py::test_an_unknown_exception_type_collapses_to_error, tests/test_metrics.py::test_an_unknown_tool_or_surface_does_not_become_a_label, tests/test_metrics.py::test_no_label_value_can_carry_caller_data, tests/test_metrics.py::test_metrics_needs_the_token_by_default, tests/test_metrics.py::test_metrics_can_be_opened_deliberately, tests/test_metrics.py::test_metrics_refuses_a_post, tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_buckets_are_cumulative, tests/test_metrics.py::test_knowledge_age_is_reported_and_the_corpus_date_only_when_there_is_one, tests/test_metrics.py::test_every_alert_names_a_metric_this_code_emits, tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_rule_refuses_without_a_service_monitor, tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_error_ratio_alert_excludes_invalid_arguments, tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_receiver_accepts_alertmanagers_own_payload_shape, ci:Chart guards (fail-closed NetworkPolicy, fsGroup only off-OpenShift, web flag wired to env)
G237 A red team on the router found that nine of the twenty printed commands could not be run, and that the guard meant to prevent exactly that passed them. G235's own row claimed every suggested command was checked against the CLI's parser. The test checked that the longest real PREFIX of the words was a command, which is the one-directional guard G206 is about: kube-noob vuln rank passed because vuln exists. Parsing all twenty showed eight that cannot run -- a missing --port, two missing positionals, three subcommands that do not exist (vuln rank, build <log>, must-gather <path>), a -f that means --values, and generate policies, which is a different command entirely -- plus rhacs compare, which parses and then exits 2 asking for -f. A suggestion that cannot be run is worse than no suggestion, and it was printed under the words "kube-noob answers this directly". The test parses them now. Nothing consulted what had been recognised, so a one-line pasted Event whose pod was called gate-7d9f was answered with the CI gate, a Deployment named upgrade with upgrade-risks, and an SBOM with a component called gate with the gate again -- while the tool description promised the field was "empty for anything pasted". It is gated on the recognised kinds now, as a SUBSET test rather than a membership test, because recognise returns no kind at all for several real typed questions and requiring the kind would have silenced those. Three causes behind every remaining false answer, each worth more than its fix. First, \b treats -, /, : and . as boundaries, so a route word inside a Kubernetes NAME matched it: a pod called ci-runner-gate-5, a Deployment called upgrade-checker, a namespace called evidence. The patterns are compiled with a boundary that requires a character which cannot appear in a name. Second, a word in TWO of a route's lookahead groups makes it fire on that one word -- which is the whole defect requiring two groups was meant to prevent. It had happened four times (fail, gate, takeover and blast radius, evidence), so it is refused at import now, and the first version of that refusal read \bfail\b as the word "bfail" and so could only ever fire by coincidence -- it still caught three real defects, which is how a guard that is right by accident goes unnoticed. Third, a sentence reporting a MALFUNCTION is a diagnosis whatever words it shares: "service ingress-nginx in namespace public crashes on the new node" is not an attack-path question. Deliberately excluding "fail" and "error" from that suppressor, because "my image build failed" and "why did my helm upgrade fail" are the two questions those routes exist for. And the cost: unanchored, search retried each pattern's lookaheads from every one of up to 600 start positions -- 75 to 92 ms per call on a 600-character line that matches nothing, paid on every one-line paste on every surface. Anchored, 1 ms. Anchoring first required finding a pattern whose alternation was written flat, so it parsed as (?=(.*A)|(B)|(C)) and only worked because search scanned; anchored, two of its three branches would silently never have matched. Six routes were added for questions the sweep found had a precise answer and no route, two were reordered because the cap is two and table order decides which leads, six why strings that oversold their command were corrected, and the question with a direct answer no longer gets the pod-triage steps printed underneath it -- which was G235's own complaint, still true of its own output tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_every_routed_command_actually_parses, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_nothing_is_routed_for_text_that_only_shares_words, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_no_word_is_in_two_lookahead_groups_of_a_route, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_every_route_is_anchored_so_the_cost_stays_linear, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_long_non_matching_line_is_cheap, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_recognised_artifact_is_not_routed_even_on_one_line, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_question_with_a_direct_answer_is_not_also_given_pod_triage_steps, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_the_better_answer_comes_first, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_carriage_returns_count_as_a_line_break, tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_pasted_denial_is_left_to_the_explainer_that_already_ran

G241 KN-S036, and the image reference that does not look like one. All three external reviews asked for a registry allowlist and the catalogue had none -- verified against the code this time, after R1 turned out to be a rule that already existed (G240). The allowlist is audit.allowedRegistries in the configuration file, and deliberately NOT a command-line flag or an MCP tool argument: which registries an organisation has approved is an organisational decision that should be the same for every caller of a deployment, not something a caller picks per request. With none configured the rule declines, which keeps it out of the evaluated list, because reporting every image as approved for want of a policy is the absence-read-as-pass failure the whole catalogue is built against. The reference parser is the content. Docker's rule is that the first path component is the registry only when it LOOKS like a host -- it contains a dot or a colon, or it is exactly localhost -- so nginx and bitnami/nginx both come from docker.io however little they look like it, and an allowlist that missed that would pass every unqualified image, which is the commonest reference there is. The finding says so in that case, because a reference that names no registry is exactly when a reader needs telling which one they are pulling from. An entry may narrow to one organisation (quay.io/my-org), which is the only useful form for a registry anyone can push to, and the match is on a path SEGMENT so quay.io/my-org-evil/app cannot impersonate it. One thing deliberately left undone and written down rather than half-built: this rule belongs in admission, and it is the only entry in NOT_GENERATED whose reason is "not yet" rather than "cannot" -- the generator's contract is that a policy fails exactly what the audit reports, so the allowlist has to reach policy generate first, and a policy emitted with an empty list would admit everything while looking like enforcement tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_the_registry_is_read_the_way_a_runtime_reads_it, tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_it_declines_rather_than_passing_everything_when_no_policy_is_configured, tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_an_entry_can_narrow_to_one_organisation, tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_a_prefix_cannot_be_impersonated_by_a_longer_name, tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_an_unqualified_image_is_measured_against_docker_hub_and_says_so, tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_an_embedded_pod_spec_is_checked_too, tests/test_policy_generate.py::test_audit_flags_the_bad_fixture_and_not_the_good
G242 A generated policy is operator input compiled into an enforcing expression, so what it cannot compile it refuses to emit. KN-S036 was the one rule in NOT_GENERATED whose reason was "not yet": the allowlist now reaches policy generate from audit.allowedRegistries and the rule is generated for both engines. Three decisions are the content. The CEL is written to agree with the audit case by case, not approximately. Every test carries its separator, so quay.io/my-org admits quay.io/my-org itself, quay.io/my-org/app:1 and quay.io/my-org@sha256:..., and refuses quay.io/my-org:1 (the audit compares the tag as part of the path), quay.io/my-org-evil/app and quay.io:443/my-org/app -- a different registry. Two kinds of allowlist are refused rather than compiled. An entry naming docker.io needs the "is the first path component a host" test, which the API server's CEL cannot do without split; an entry whose host has no dot, port or localhost is never a registry to a runtime, so myregistry/app comes from Docker Hub and a startsWith('myregistry/') policy would ADMIT an image the audit reports. Both are stated in the file header instead, because a policy that disagrees with the audit is worse than no policy, and an unquotable string reaching an enforcing expression is worse still -- a policy that fails to compile denies every request under failurePolicy: Fail. An empty list still generates nothing, for the same reason the audit declines: enforcement that enforces nothing is the absence-read-as-a-pass failure in its most expensive form. The agreement is asserted, not asserted twice: the parity test compares the policy's verdict with the audit's over 80 reference shapes and four allowlists, and the 2,700-object random cross-check now runs with an allowlist so the rule is in it. CI's kind cluster generates it through a written config file, so the one expression built from operator-supplied strings is the one a real API server type-checks tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_the_generated_policy_refuses_exactly_what_the_audit_reports, tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_an_allowlist_the_generator_cannot_compile_emits_nothing, tests/test_policy_generate.py::test_registry_rule_is_generated_only_with_an_allowlist, tests/test_policy_generate.py::test_every_rule_is_generated_or_has_a_reason, tests/test_policy_generate.py::test_policy_equals_audit_on_random_objects, ci:Generated policies on the API server

G245 The same image, written three ways, has to get one verdict -- and the audit was the side that was wrong. G247 A guard against over-matching became a guard against matching. A sweep of forty questions a platform engineer would type then found one more wrong answer and seven missing ones. The wrong one: "my pod is being denied by pod security admission" went to the NetworkPolicy reachability checker, because pod security put pod in the sentence twice and \bpod\b.*\bpod\b was all that route needed -- it refuses an admission denial now, which policy explain answers. The missing ones each had an exact command: the rule catalogue, whether the cluster records API requests (the G244 block), an image from an unapproved registry or on a mutable tag, watching a namespace for incidents, an OCPBUGS key, whether the audit reads the KServe and Ray CRDs, and the training bench. Every new route was then attacked with text sharing its words -- "check all pods", "image pull failed from quay", "the namespace has a watch annotation" -- and tightened until none routes; the catalogue route's phrases are written out flat because (?:rule|check) nested inside one makes the import guard read a bare check as its word, and that refusal is correct. Two defects in the question router, and they are each other's mirror. One: "which secrets are exposed in the public repo?" routed to posture scan, which walks Routes, ServiceAccounts and RBAC in a cluster -- the question is about files in Git. It routes to audit now, where KN-S018 and KN-S026 are, and the exposure route carries a negative lookahead for repository words, so the wrong answer is not merely ranked second behind the right one (the cap of two would have hidden it). Two: "is anything in my cluster exposed to the internet?" -- the plainest form of the question posture scan exists for -- matched NOTHING, because internet and public sat in the route's first word group, and _refuse_a_word_in_two_groups forbids the second group from holding them too, so no sentence could satisfy both groups with those words alone. The fix is phrases in the second group (internet-facing, publicly, to the internet, from outside, outside the cluster) rather than the bare nouns, which also keeps a log line reading "no route to host" and a namespace called public out of it. G251 A line number that is not a line of any file somebody can open is a wrong answer, not a vague one. The findings deserved the same. The input pipeline now records where each file starts in the combined text -- counted off the text that precedes it, separator included, because whether a file ends with a newline changes the arithmetic and a number that is right for most inputs is the defect being replaced -- and each finding is put back in its own file before anything is printed, emitted, compacted or turned into OSCAL. G254 A chart is a claim nobody checks the arithmetic on, so it may only restate one. The HTML report (U1) grew charts, and every rule about them follows from that sentence. A chart shows only what a table on the same page shows: the severity bars and the trend each ship with the table they are drawn from, which is why adding them added two tables the page did not have. Colour is never the only carrier -- every bar is labelled with its name and its count beside the mark, because the page is read in print, in forced colours and by someone with no colour vision; the palettes were run through a colour-vision validator (lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair separation, contrast against the surface) in both light and dark, which is how the amber excepted bucket became teal: next to the red findings bucket it failed the separation check that the two buckets a reader must never confuse are exactly the ones to get right. And an absence is never drawn as a zero -- an empty bar and a missing bar are the same picture, so the trend is withheld entirely when any snapshot in the series could not read every resource, which is the rule posture report already applies to control standing for the same reason: a line through a floor publishes a fall in findings that may only be a fall in what kube-noob was allowed to see. The geometry is integer arithmetic over the counts, so the tests assert that twice the findings is twice the bar instead of looking at a picture, and the escaping rule of the page applies one layer down, inside the SVG. The red team of the first version found the rule's sharpest case: every report written before 0.8 carries no notRead key, under the SAME format string, so reading a missing key as "nothing was unread" marked a folder inherited across an upgrade as fourteen complete reads. G258 "Does not do the old thing" is not "does the new thing", and a renamed field is a wrong answer rather than a refused one. A renamed or restructured field is never read by the nearest older shape; it gets a reader that knows the new one, or the document is refused by name. The same rule in the CNI table: Calico 3.32 dropping AdminNetworkPolicy and Calico 3.32 enforcing ClusterNetworkPolicy are two sourced facts, and deriving one from the other would read every CNI that enforces neither as enforcing the new kind. G261 A guard that cannot pass is not a guard, and a flag bundle in quotes is one argument. ci/helm-and-dogfood held PR #47 red, and the step was the registry-allowlist guard G243 added -- the one that proves audit.allowedRegistries reaches both config files, the sidecar's and the webhook's, so kube-noob audit and the admission webhook give one verdict. G263 A wall-clock budget is a proxy for a property, and it is the proxy that depends on the machine. test_a_padded_object_does_not_outrun_the_webhooks_timeout asserted elapsed < 5.0 for 4,000 containers. It held for months, then measured 5.44 s on one shared 3.11 runner and failed -- on the 0.9.0 release commit, while 3.12, 3.13 and 3.14 passed the same code. Locally that object costs 0.86 s, so the budget had no headroom on a slow runner and the verdict was about the machine, not the code. The test is named for a shape, and its own docstring says the fix "makes the cost linear": quadratic in four times the input is about sixteen times the time, linear about four. So it measures 1,000 and 4,000 back to back on the same machine and asserts the ratio stays under 8 -- a slow runner slows both halves and the ratio holds. Proved rather than assumed: with G252's memo disabled, the ratio is 15.7x (1,000 = 2.01 s, 4,000 = 31.46 s) and the new assertion fires, so it catches the regression the budget caught while no longer catching the runner. A warm-up call comes first, because the cold call pays for imports -- 500 containers cost 1.33 s cold against 0.2 s warm, which would make the small sample carry the startup and the ratio read as sub-linear. test_the_memory_demo_runs_offline_under_a_second had the same shape (1.32 s against a 1.0 s budget, passing 3/3 when idle) and its ceiling is now loose enough that only a hang trips it, with the contract left to the assertions on what the demo printed. The rule: assert the invariant, and give a clock only a ceiling a catastrophe would have to cross tests/test_admission.py::test_a_padded_object_does_not_outrun_the_webhooks_timeout, tests/test_memory_repo.py::test_the_memory_demo_runs_offline_under_a_second

G265 A check that runs before the thing it checks is not a check, and dist/ is the publish action's whole argument. The pinned pypa/gh-action-pypi-publish runs twine check dist/* and then exec twine upload dist/* over the WHOLE directory. twine 7.0.0 rejects anything that is not a Python distribution -- sbom.spdx.json with "Unknown distribution format", the krew archive with "No PKG-INFO in archive" -- and aborts before it opens a connection. From cf08981 (2026-11-05) both were copied into dist/, purely so github-release would attach them. v0.7.1 shipped before that; v0.8.0 died earlier on a missing PyYAML; v0.9.0 died one step earlier still on a Rekor 404. So publish-pypi had never once run with those files present, and the first release that got past attestation would have failed with nothing published. Two blind spots hid it. scripts/check_publishable.sh is the deliberate twin of the action's own check -- same action SHA, same Python, same bundled twine -- and it ran BEFORE the two steps that contaminated dist/, so it was checking a directory that no longer existed by the time the action read it. And ci.yml's publishable job builds the dists without ever running the SBOM or krew steps, so no pull request could see it either: the sequence existed only in release.yml and was first executed on a tag. Non-distribution release assets live in assets/ now, check_publishable.sh runs last over the dist/ the action will actually see, and three tests hold it: nothing but a distribution is written into dist/, the publishable check is ordered after every step that touches it, and provenance is attempted twice while still deciding the job. Reproduced locally with the action's exact twine before changing anything: twine check dist/* exit 1, twine upload dist/* exit 1 tests/test_workflow_honesty.py::test_nothing_but_a_python_distribution_is_written_into_dist, tests/test_workflow_honesty.py::test_the_publishable_check_runs_after_everything_that_touches_dist, tests/test_workflow_honesty.py::test_provenance_is_attempted_twice_and_still_decides_the_job

G268 The one artifact that leaves for someone else's pipeline named a schema nothing here had ever read. gate --sarif writes "$schema": "https://json.schemastore.org/sarif-2.1.0.json" into every file it hands to GitHub code scanning, and no test, no gate and no CI step had ever checked a single document against it -- in a tool whose argument is that it works with no network. The document turns out to be valid, which is the good case and not the point: it was valid by luck, and the first field added to it would have been checked by whoever consumed it, in their pipeline, not here. The schema is now vendored from OASIS, the committee that owns SARIF, rather than from schemastore, which republishes it and is not reachable from a locked-down build; python scripts/vendor_schemas.py --sarif refetches it with its upstream sha256 beside it, which is more than the OSCAL schema next to it can say. The URI in the output is unchanged, because code-scanning consumers recognise it and editing a published contract to match a test is the wrong way round. Two things had to be got right to make the check mean anything: SARIF 2.1.0 declares draft-04 and uses the draft-04 id keyword, which ajv 8 refuses outright (NOT SUPPORTED: keyword "id"), so the validator picks ajv-draft-04 on the dialect the schema declares rather than rewriting the schema to a draft the committee did not publish -- and a test asserts the dialect, so a re-vendor cannot silently fall through to the other compiler; and a second test mutates one level to severe and requires the validator to refuse it, because a validator that compiles a schema to nothing and waves everything through passes exactly as quietly as one that works. oscal_validate.mjs became json_schema_validate.mjs: one validator, two dialects, rather than a second copy of thirty lines tests/test_gate_sarif.py::test_the_gate_writes_sarif_that_validates_against_the_oasis_schema, tests/test_gate_sarif.py::test_the_validator_refuses_a_document_the_schema_does_not_allow, tests/test_gate_sarif.py::test_the_schema_is_the_draft_the_validator_branches_on, tests/test_gate_sarif.py::test_the_schema_is_vendored_with_its_provenance
G269 A SARIF file can only say "nothing found" by being short, so the audit's caveats have to travel in it. audit --format sarif finishes I2. The format is a list of results, and an audit that was handed one Pod from a namespace declines its cross-object rules, and one that meets a file it cannot parse skips it; both produce a shorter list and nothing else, which whatever reads the file next takes for a cleaner cluster. The JSON report says so in assumptions and unreadable, and a format flag that REPLACES the report would have left both behind. They go into invocations[].toolExecutionNotifications instead -- warnings for what was not evaluated, errors for what was not read -- with executionSuccessful still true, because the tool ran and what it could not decide is a notification, not a crash. Every rule, in the gate's file and the audit's, now carries the NIST SP 800-53 controls the catalogue maps it to, twice on purpose: NIST-800-53/AC-6 tags for a code-scanning view to filter on and a nist-800-53 list for a program to read; a rule mapped to no control has NO such property, because an empty list reads as "checked, maps to nothing". Two refusals came with it. --format is shared by every command, so search --format sarif parsed and would have printed YAML under the name: any command but audit now exits 2. And --oscal --format sarif asked for two documents and got OSCAL as YAML: also exit 2. The exit code of the audit itself is unchanged by the flag, as with --oscal and --compact. G270 Which operators are installed is a question whose most dangerous answer is an empty list -- or a short one. E2. An inventory that could not see the OLM objects and printed nothing would read exactly like a cluster with no operators, and one that dropped a file it could not parse would read exactly like a cluster without that operator; the next reader (R5 matching versions against known issues, an upgrade pre-mortem, an assessor) acts on what is missing. So there are four verdicts and only one of them lists: listed means nothing the answer needs was skipped, partial names what was, not-determined means no ClusterServiceVersion was read, and no-olm means both live reads got a 404 for operators.coreos.com in the exact words the backends write for a missing resource type -- not any message containing "not found", which is how an auth plugin's error once read as "not an OLM cluster". The first version was red-teamed and 21 holes were found, almost all the same one: a skipped, unreadable, truncated, foreign-kind or status-less input reading as an answer. Each is a gap now: a file in a directory that does not parse, an OLM file the must-gather reader skipped or found outside the layout oc adm inspect writes, an empty file, an error message or Status in an OLM path, a list item that is not its kind, objects past the cap, a Subscription of another API group (ACM, Knative), conflicting duplicates (resolved by content, so order never decides), a response with no items, a Subscription with no status, and a namespace with CSVs and no Subscription in a file set. The join was the other half: a Subscription joins on installedCSV alone, to an original CSV in its own namespace, so a copy never fabricates an install, currentCSV is only ever the upgrade in flight, and one CSV is one operator however many Subscriptions name it; copies fold into their original only when version and phase agree. A phase that is absent or not one OLM defines is unknown and counted as not Succeeded. Shown strings are made printable by Unicode category (sanitize.visible), which the must-gather analysers share. The text view prints what the JSON carries (G221), must-gather analyze carries the inventory in both, and --require-complete exits 5 on anything but listed or no-olm G273 A blast radius is only as honest as the legs it admits it could not trace -- and the first version of this one was not. posture reach (V3) starts at one workload and answers five legs, each reached, none or not determined: how it is exposed, the ServiceAccount token its pods hold (automounted, or projected by a volume -- automountServiceAccountToken: false does not stop the second), the bindings that name the account (directly, or through system:serviceaccounts, system:serviceaccounts:<ns>, system:authenticated or the system:serviceaccount: User) and what each allows, the Secrets it may read by RBAC or reach by creating a pod (names and scopes only), and the node when the pod spec can escape to it. One view, one implementation: every edge comes from the attack-path scan's own reach and carries the evidence line posture scan prints for that step plus the object and field that prove it; the inventory is put in one canonical order inside the scan, so scan and reach name the same proof and the scan is byte-stable under shuffled input (it was not, on main either). The object named is scored on its own spec, whatever its kind -- a Pod because it is what runs, an old ReplicaSet or a CronJob's Job because it can differ from its owner's template -- with its owner chain and what differs from the top owner (account, node escape, the differing images, the labels) as context; two different objects with the anchor's name are an error rather than a pick, an ambiguous owner a note. An adversarial review of the first version found seventeen defects before merge, the worst a truncated graph that cut a privileged cluster-admin pod's capabilities and then summarised the remainder as "reaches no capability". The rules that now hold, each with a test that failed on that version: the summary comes from the whole graph and truncation keeps the strongest capabilities, the node escape and the gaps first, a binding only where something kept needs it; the document is bounded in bytes as well as count (96 KB for an MCP caller, diagram included) and says what it withheld; not determined is never written none -- a ServiceAccount, Role or aggregated ClusterRole the input does not carry, a stand-in for admin, edit or view (they aggregate aggregate-to-* ClusterRoles, which are applied when the bundle has them, through chains, whatever the label names), a duplicated name, a rules, subjects or verbs that is not a list, a bound resource and verb kube-noob does not classify (rule by rule, pair by pair, counted and named), a projected token for another audience, and a refused or namespace-limited live read each become a node with the reason; files are never complete, because a set of files cannot show the bindings or Services it leaves out, so none is said of a file only where the pod spec decides it, unless --whole-bundle asserts the files are everything that applies, and --require-complete follows that; the node leg reads the audit's own dangerous-capability list, an unmasked /proc and Windows HostProcess, its none names what it checked, and hostPort is deliberately not an escape (it is an exposure); every text field is one line and every Mermaid label escaped, so a newline or a quote in a name forges neither a row nor a click statement, and the limitations are printed in text and drawn as note nodes. The scan is corrected in the same primitives: it now ranks the escalation rights the Kubernetes RBAC good-practices and authorization pages name -- TokenRequest, Secret creation with a way to use the token, exec by get, ephemeral containers, PersistentVolume creation, webhook control, CSR creation with approval -- each pinned to its page in attack_paths._api_capabilities; a cross-namespace RoleBinding through a group is no longer skipped and its path names that namespace; measured on every fixture, seeded.yaml gains one score-5 path and nothing else moves. A second review found that six fixes had moved the defect, and nine more defects (N1-N12, R1-R3); each now has a failing-first test and the neighbour of each was tried. RBAC matches as the API server matches (ResourceMatches: *, the name, */<sub>), each rule is classified resource by verb, and harmless is a named list with a source per entry (reviews, token reviews, leases, events, status writes, role writes without bind/escalate, OpenShift scope impersonation, a TokenRequest for itself) -- 8 of 10 legs determined on a realistic app, operator and bootstrap set, from 6; secrets create needs a way to use the token; full control does not list the rights inside it again, so cluster-admin keeps main's six paths. Gateway API HTTPRoutes and Istio VirtualServices are exposures and are on the live allowlist and the chart's read Role; other routing kinds, a front to a Service not in the files, an RBAC object not read, an unreadable subject, a refused SCC list for a pod creator, a token Secret at the token path not in the files, and containers that is not a list are not determined; hostPort and hostNetwork are exposure. Legacy authorization.openshift.io RBAC is read as the objects it proxies. Duplicates: Roles not read, Services each followed and named, workloads each scored and placed apart, the same object twice counted once. Every list in the document is bounded, open legs first, and no input exceeds the byte cap. The scan diff against main over every fixture and 112 stress files is classified in the CHANGELOG; KN-S010 shares the scan's node-takeover list. A third review found ten more. A failed live list -- 404 included, since an Istio before 1.22 or a Gateway API before v1.0 answers it for the version read, and kube-noob reads no discovery -- is never an absence (notServed removed; 18 of 25 legs determined live on plain Kubernetes, from 22); --read-namespace leaves exposure open to routes from namespaces not read, and unmodelled routes count at their backends; same-name workloads are kept (a copy's cluster-admin token was dropped for the other's hostPID); token Secrets by projection, env or envFrom; VirtualService delegates and mirrors; route header values masked in cluster_get; the summary follows the exposure leg; reach names the scan's duplicates; malformed exposure fields are unknowns; server-set fields do not make two objects. KN-S010's critical set is the credential and API paths only (/etc/kubernetes, /var/lib/kubelet/pki and /pods, /root, /var/lib/etcd and below, runtime sockets anywhere), not every path under /etc or /run, which made gate --fail-on critical fail on kube-proxy and every CNI; the exit-code changes are listed in the CHANGELOG. A fourth review found ten more. Objects without a namespace go where -n puts them, as kubectl apply -n does, and without it their account legs are not determined (they were placed in default, and Argo CD's controller read "read Secrets (default)"); same-name copies are placed apart, never merged (the round-three union joined one copy's vulnerable image to the other's cluster-admin token: 58.8 where main said 29.4), with ambiguous in rank, gate and assess output; HTTPRoute mirrors, GRPCRoutes (read live too), non-Service backends, selectorless and ExternalName Services and bad Service types are modelled or not determined; live exposure is never none; route query parameters, Route header actions and Ingress credential snippets are masked; KN-S010 adds runtime socket directories, their ancestors and /proc/<pid>/root, which makes /run and /var/run critical. A fifth review found six more. Unplaced fronts and bindings open exposure and RBAC (the workload and its account were the only ones checked); copies are ref#n everywhere and counted once each, with the differing fields named; -n reaches vuln report, code report, gate and the MCP tools; more credential carriers are masked; KN-S010 carves /etc/kubernetes/node-feature-discovery and /etc/kubernetes/secrets-store-csi-providers out G274 An alert becomes an evidence report the server can keep, the time it took is measured, and nothing a sender writes can stretch it, sink it or steer it (I1). POST /v1/alert-reports takes Alertmanager's own payload and returns one report per alert, each section made by the diagnose tool called with that alert alone (signals, playbook diagnosis, reported issues for the release that alert's own label names, approved team memory, read-only next steps), so the report cannot drift from what the tools answer. Every section states what it could not determine: a release outside the local issues, or a memory repository that could not be read, is not-evaluated, never an empty match. An alert with no recognised signal is kept and never called healthy; a resolved alert is recorded, not diagnosed; an alert whose diagnosis failed or that the time budget (alertReports.budgetSeconds) did not reach keeps a not-evaluated row and is named; items that are not alerts and alerts past 500 are counted. A size cap keeps a row for every alert and takes detail from resolved and low-severity alerts first, naming them; the summary is computed before it. Names in suggested commands come only from the alert's own labels and only when they are valid Kubernetes names, in the report and in plain diagnose of a payload. Values are redacted before they are clipped and lose their scheme:// URLs; the shared redactor's flag pattern runs only near a keyword (same output, linear time), and the body is read, built and rendered off the event loop, two reports at a time. The HTTP server alone writes reports, and only with alertReports.store (the chart refuses it with more than one replica): owner-only, atomic, never through a link, bounded by count, age and size; retention never removes the report just written and counts what it cannot remove; ids are checked against [0-9] and \Z; read paths change nothing; a stored file is type-checked when read back and a damaged one is a 500. Storage off or unwritable is stored: false with the reason. The safety contract fails on any boolean that defaults to on without a reviewed line. /v1/reports stays the posture report. Second review: a post waits at most 5 s for one of two report slots, shared with the MCP tool, and is then answered 503 with Retry-After (an MCP call gets a busy tool error); the budget counts from receipt; a post whose sender has gone is neither built nor stored. A lone private-key header no longer erases the rest of the text in any tool that redacts, prose about API keys is left alone, the JWT and private-key scans are linear (counted, not timed), and PRIVACY.md lists what the redactor removes and what it does not. An exporter's service label does not name the service in a step; labels set only the namespace and pod, so a forbidden message's verb and user, a pull secret and a claim still come from the text; diagnose and the report read one version precedence. An entry that is not a regular file is not listed, read or counted toward the cap, and a file with a second link is not served. The safety contract also fails an unreviewed Literal, URL or non-empty list default, and a boolean inside a list of models. Third review: every request body must arrive within 10 s (408); a report slot is taken only after the body is read, on the event loop for REST and MCP alike, so a sender that trickles holds no slot and a waiting report holds no worker thread; a body counts as large only once it is in memory, waits for room until its read deadline, and frees its place when its tool takes it; a refusal on /mcp is a JSON-RPC error; an exporter's own pod and namespace are not the subject, exported_* labels are when the plain ones name the exporter, TargetDown names its target; an application's version label is not an OpenShift release; a reference to a Secret is not redacted where the text proves it is one (the describe form, secret '…' key '…', secretKeyRef), while a plain password=x/secret is; an exported_* label is the subject whatever the exporter; --access-key and cut keys behind log prefixes or escaped newlines are; the safety contract walks dict and Optional models, runs validators, and flags non-first Literal choices and bare hosts tests/test_alert_report.py::test_the_report_is_the_diagnose_tools_answer_not_a_second_implementation, G276 A plugin that can only describe the engine is not the product, and a skill that answers without it is the failure this project exists to stop. The directory takes Markdown, JSON and images from an organization that is not Verified and refuses a local MCP server, so kube-noob-skills could not run one audit. Read against Anthropic's published rules on 2026-11-12, three designs were admissible: skills that drive the CLI the user installed through Claude's Bash tool ("a script that SKILL.md only tells Claude to run isn't part of this check"); a remote server at the user's own kube-noob serve through userConfig, which Claude Code reports as Invalid MCP server config ... URL is unset or invalid on every install that never runs serve; and hooks, bin/ or !-command lines, which chat and Cowork refuse or the validator follows as code. The first was built; the second is written up for the owner. An adversarial review of the first version then found twenty defects, and the worst were the project's own thesis in new clothes. A crash exited 1, which is "findings at or above --fail-on": a wrong-typed field (resources: 1 in a Role) crashed audit, redteam, fix, vuln threat, protocol and posture scan, so a pipeline read a crash as a violation and the skill read it as an answer. A rule that cannot read a field's shape is now not evaluated and named. The hand-written readers outside the rules go through kube_noob.shape, which raises one exception naming the field: a red-team scenario then becomes undecided, fix leaves the document untouched with a manual follow-up, and the threat model and attack paths report the object under notRead. Anything still escaping main exits 4. The skills read exit 0 as "nothing found" when most commands exit 0 with findings, and missed half the names commands use for what they did not read. Each skill now carries a per-command table, checked by running every command on a fixture. The CLI's JSON reached the model uncleaned, with its not-read keys after a tool's output cap: KUBE_NOOB_FOR_MODEL=1 reuses kube_noob.sanitize, stamps knowledgeAsOf and puts those keys first, and the skills write output to a file and print the exit code. Pasted text went through a fixed heredoc delimiter, so a pasted line could end it and run as a command; it is now written with the file tool into a private mktemp -d folder. Ordinary checks counted ids in the data folder; the skills now run with KUBE_NOOB_LEARNING_ENABLED=false. kubectl noob and the uvx fallback could download without consent and draw a reviewer hold; the skills use only an installed kube-noob. Every command line is parsed with parse_args. On krew: the archive carries LICENSE, uses stored gzip blocks so its digest no longer depends on the zlib build, describes no commands, and names the documented tap; the no-krew install stops on a failed digest, and the krew-index steps refuse an archive without LICENSE A second review found an absence still read as a pass: a PodDisruptionBudget with selector: 7 made KN-C009 decline and RT-UPGRADE-BLOCK, reading only KN-C009, called the upgrade path mitigated. A scenario now records the rules it reads and is undecided, naming them, when one did not run; a rule that fails on one object runs again without it; posture scan ranks an object with unreadable metadata under a name that says so; a kind that is not a string is named as not audited; and the skills need kube-noob 0.9.3, the first release with all of this, and call an older one too old. A third review found the exit 5 fired on every CRD a cluster does not serve, so no live gate could pass: it now fires on what was refused (wrong shape, 403, timeout, not JSON), and on what was absent only with --require-complete. It also found the red-team record shared between threads, a PodDisruptionBudget of the wrong shape read as no PDB, an unbounded and order-dependent note, fix overwriting securityContext: 7, documents no rule reads counted as audited, a Rollout with spec: 7 dropped, and a phrase starting with - parsed as an option; each is fixed and pinned. Merged with G273 and G279, which had rewritten the inventory under it: Inventory.unread(entry, cause) is the one rule deciding which of notRead and refused an entry goes to, every site names a cause from a closed list, a table-driven test drives every site through posture scan and posture reach and a source check refuses a site that writes to a list directly; a live list is refused when the cluster forbade it, timed out, answered something that is not a list (G279's structural check) or not JSON, and an absence when the backend's own words say the type is not served -- for an aggregated group too, which the verdict still keeps not determined, so the kind gate passes on a cluster with no Routes; posture reach carries refused, notReadWithheld and refusedWithheld in JSON and text and G277 The first five minutes must not end in a false all-clear, a missing file or a wall of YAML. A user-side review ran the documented first-run path and hit four defects before any feature mattered. (1) known-issues --version 4.16.20 answered "matches": [] with exit 0 and only a prose note naming the indexed releases, so a caller read "not a known bug": the P6 defect class (a verdict earned by absence) in another table. G278 A join the data cannot populate is coverage theatre, so R5 shipped as a release-note lookup that says what it cannot decide -- and two reviews found fifteen, then a dozen more, ways it still overstated. Measured first: 957 records (921 issue ids) for OpenShift 4.20-4.22, keyed by OpenShift release and text; none carries a component, a package or an operator version, and every fix is an OpenShift release, while an operator OpenShift ships is versioned 4.y.0-<build> and updated by OLM on its own schedule. operators issues matches each installed operator's package through ALIASES, in two tiers that are never merged: the product's name (named) and component names or abbreviations (mentioned); an issue that also names another product or a platform component is a mention, with the reason. 72 records (65 issues) are found for 13 packages, 27 records name the product outright. The verdict says whether anything was matched (matched, partly-matched, not-matched), separately from whether the evidence was complete (partial, not-determined): thirty operators without an alias used to read assessed and pass --require-complete. complete is exactly not incompleteBecause, one list, so the flag and its reasons cannot disagree (they did three ways); a cluster the notes do not cover -- not OpenShift, OKD, a release outside the index -- is incomplete with that reason, and a release reason is not added when there is nothing to look up. allMatched is true only for matched, never vacuously (no installed operator, no OLM). There is no matching exit code: --require-matched reused exit 5 (unread evidence) for a gap in kube-noob's own name table and was removed; jq -e '.allMatched == true' is the gate. A known issue stays listed at later releases until the fix it lists, including a fix in a later release's z-stream (known_issues.Index._applies); open issues come first and truncation is counted per class; a fix is never decided, only compared with the cluster's version; community, certified and marketplace builds are not looked up and other catalogs are unverified; the ClusterVersion named version is read even when a version is given, an update in progress searches both releases and is incomplete whether or not a version is given, releasesSearched and the one update caveat list only what was searched, a version from status.desired says so, a cluster that does not serve ClusterVersion is not OpenShift, and an OKD version is not OpenShift Container Platform; a must-gather whose ClusterVersion is not named version stays OpenShift, says so, and keeps its condition findings; the must-gather report, its case draft and the analyser's text decide OKD through the same operator_issues.okd_version and say the same okd_note, with knownIssuesVerdict not-determined instead of OpenShift matches; a name a refreshed corpus no longer uses makes the operator not determined. The loader every known-issue consumer shares now checks each record of a local copy and counts what it leaves out; the bundled snapshot loads unchanged. Not a gate, not a vulnerability check: the next step is the local Jira copy's components and fix versions G281 A rule about a kind that is absent from the input is not evaluated, and never clean. The coverage record counted a rule as evaluated when its check ran, whatever it ran over, so audit -f examples/kube-noob-exceptions.yaml recorded 87 of 93 rules as evaluated on one ExceptionList and the OSCAL document built from a Deployment-only directory reported 15 controls satisfied; a platform-gated rule read as clean on every bundle of the other platform. Every rule now DECLARES its subject (subjects= on @rule: a kind, alternatives, the pod-spec kinds, or every object; context= for kinds it reads beside the subject), and a rule whose subject is not in the input does not run: notEvaluated with the reason "no <Kind> in the input", whatever else it could have read (a rule that reads its context first and its subject second was "evaluated without" under the first, inferred version). A kind counts only in an API group the rules know it in (SUBJECT_GROUPS; by_kind, has_subject and workloads filter the same way), so a same-named kind from another group is not the subject and the reason carries the group (no operators.coreos.com Subscription in the input). That set is every group the kind has ever been served under, derived from REMOVED_APIS (an extensions/v1beta1 Deployment is a Deployment; the first version refused it, so a legacy bundle lost its findings and gained a false KN-V011 while the Service beside the dropped Deployment was read), and no apiVersion at all is an unknown, not a contradiction (API list items carry none, so a live snapshot evaluated 3 rules where 0.9.2 fired 19; every live collector -- posture snapshot, attack-path scan, operator inventory -- stamps the apiVersion the object was listed under through one helper, keeping the backend's own when it has one). OpenShift 3.x's ungrouped v1 DeploymentConfig, Route, BuildConfig, Build, ImageStream, Template, Project, SecurityContextConstraints and the authorization kinds are read as those kinds (LEGACY_OPENSHIFT_V1, from openshift/api release-3.11 legacy.go), for exactly those kinds; KN-V002 still fires on them. A workload-kind document in a group the rules do not know is named in the assumptions as not audited, and KN-V011/KN-N012 decline for its namespace rather than conclude "selects nothing" from pods they did not read. KN-V010 declares the four apps kinds it judges and KN-N001 the long-running kinds and Pod it fires for, so neither is evaluated-and-clean on a CronJob-only or Pod-only input; KN-ST001 declares the StorageClass it reads as context, and a test walks every rule body for by_kind and fails when the kind is declared as neither; the pod-spec kinds are spelled out inside an alternative label too. The text view's controls with findings line names only controls with findings > 0 (it listed every touched control with its count, so a control whose only rule was not evaluated read AU-2 (0)), and a controls not evaluated, in whole or in part line names each control with a rule not evaluated, the rules, and its clean count when it is partly both, the same set the JSON's notEvaluated names. A refused object is never a pass (review 4): an object of a known kind whose apiVersion is not a string, not shaped like an API, or in a group the kind is not known under is listed under notAudited with a fixed reason, printed in the text view, carried by the gate's JSON, Markdown ("Not passed: N object(s) not audited"), HTML and SARIF (invocation property plus one note result per object) and the snapshot's coverage, the subject reason says 1 Deployment not audited (...) and never "no pod spec in the input", and audit and gate exit 1 whatever --fail-on says. apiVersion: null is a missing apiVersion; a non-string one is refused by shape, never shown as a Python repr; a reason holds no input text (a bidi override in a group name stays out of why); KN-V011/KN-N012 decline for pods in a kind with no reader as for a refused workload; OpenShift 3.x bindings' userNames/groupNames and SystemUser/SystemGroup subjects are read through the one shared conversion; the declaration walk fails loudly on an unclassifiable traversal; live-snapshot reasons never say "in the input"; KN-V014 keys duplicates on the kind's identity (one object across the groups a kind was served under, the group as well otherwise). Review 5: a pod spec in a kind with no reader and a kind that is not a kind name are not audited through the ONE helper the subject reasons read (notAudited is non-empty whenever a reason says "not audited"); the verdict is one predicate, Report.undetermined(), read by summary.score: null/summary.verdict, the text headline, the gate's "incomplete" headline, fix.after, ksi (exit 1) and fedramp_ksi, and the posture report; an ExceptionList notAudited: entry accepts one object (acceptedBy, verdict determined); the row's object is cleaned once where it is built; an unknown kind in an unknown group is neither audited nor "not audited"; legacy binding names are refused by shape and userNames: [] says why nobody is bound; posture scan reads OpenShift 3.x v1 RBAC through the shared conversion; a non-string apiVersion gets one message; the SARIF note has a location and a fingerprint. Subject labels are sorted, so the bytes do not depend on PYTHONHASHSEED; a rule that declined on one object and fired on another is evaluated with the decline as a note, never in both firedRules and notEvaluated; every cannot_decide carries the rule's own reason, a platform gate says "does not apply on <platform>", and the three buckets partition the rule set. KN-N001 still fires on a whole namespace whose workloads no NetworkPolicy selects. The reasons are in every embedding of the record, with the counts and the names agreeing per control: controlSummary.rulesNotEvaluated and per-control notEvaluatedRules in audit (JSON, YAML, text grouped by reason with the JSON's count, and one SARIF toolExecutionNotification per reason), audit_manifests (with and without an exceptions file, compact or not), fedramp_ksi and ksi (JSON, text and the HTML page, which says "Not evaluated" with the reasons), the gate (JSON, Markdown summary and HTML coverage line), the OSCAL selection remarks, a posture snapshot's coverage.notEvaluated (naming the resource when the cluster refused it) and the posture report's standing in JSON, Markdown and HTML. A record whose caller kept no reasons still NAMES the rules that did not run, each with the record's own "no reason" phrase rather than one invented for it; a diff against a 0.9.2 snapshot reports nothing regressed, the bytes do not depend on document order, no input-derived text reaches a reason, and the exit code is unchanged. G280 A check before an action that an agent may skip, or that reads "could not tell" as "fine", is not a check. kube-noob verify answers allow, deny or not-determined for a change given as manifests or as the command line an agent intends to run, and an unread input is never an allow: a URL, -f - with no input, exec, a delete of anything but pods by name, a strategic-merge patch, --context, --kuberc, KUBECONFIG or KUBECTL_EXTERNAL_DIFF on the line, a flag or subcommand it does not know, a value left over after a flag kubectl reads only with = (--validate, --dry-run, --cascade), a file outside the tree or behind a link, a file the audit skipped, a file an earlier command on the same line may have written, a pod spec in a kind no rule reads, a deployer it does not model (helmfile, argocd, flux, terraform ...), a kustomize --enable-* build, and a line its shell reader cannot follow (a command word built at run time, a substitution in ${...}, $((...)) or an unquoted here-document, code piped into a shell, a kube tool inside python -c, ssh, awk, git -c, vim -c or env -S) are all not determined, with the reason in text, JSON, YAML, the verify_change tool and the hook message alike. The reader sees through the wrappers agents use (sh -c, eval of a literal, env, sudo, timeout, xargs, find -exec, quoting, $'...', case and version variants of the tool's name, a full path, the k alias, cd, Makefile and package.json recipes, a script in the tree), never runs the command and never reads outside the working directory. -f DIR is read the way kubectl reads it (dotfiles and .json included, exact extensions, subdirectories only with -R). A dry run is the last --dry-run before --, and none/false is not one, in diagnose too. A day-2 change that cannot add a manifest finding (a restart, a scale, a label without a security key, deleting pods by name, a patch by name touching only safe fields) is allowed; a literal credential in set env is denied. The manifests are judged by gate.run, so verify allows exactly when the gate passes and denies whenever it fails on findings, on every rule fixture; exit codes add no meaning (0 allow, 1 deny, 5 not determined unless --allow-undetermined; a deny is never relaxed). helm is run only from an absolute PATH entry outside the tree, never on a chart with a link in it, with its output and its own words bounded and framed, in the gate too. kube-noob hook claude-code blocks with exit 2 on deny and on its own errors, asks the user (PreToolUse ask) on not-determined, answers inside its own deadline whatever the input, frames repository text as data and flags what reads as an instruction, asks for a PowerShell command that names a kube tool, never prints a JSON allow, and is turned off only from Claude Code's own environment. hook install --write merges atomically, refusing links, read-only files, repeated keys and a different kube-noob entry; the plugin's copy is off until the user opts in, is a sh guard that starts no program while off, and while on blocks by itself (exit 2) when uv is missing or fails, so it needs no onFailure and uv's absence or start-up reaches only users who turned it on; the hook install entry keeps onFailure: block. Second review: the reader's default is opaque -- a program it does not model is checked for a kube command in its arguments and for text naming a kube tool on its standard input, whatever it is called, so the next unlisted wrapper is not an allow, while one that names no kube command still passes; make is read through -f, -C, includes, $(shell), inline and continued recipes and command-line variables, npm-family scripts in the package their options name, sudo and timeout options by arity, and function f {}; cloud-identity annotations, --external-ip, oc expose svc, --from-file outside the tree and helm plugin install ask, and a URL with a password in set env denies. The deadline is a child process killed at the budget, the lexer's patterns are linear and a Makefile line over 4,096 characters is not read, and the plugin guard kills a uv run at 45 s. Every input-derived token in a message is quoted between « ». Third review: make reads every reachable target up to 5,000 and says when it stops; sudo -s/-i run a shell command line; just, go-task and deno tasks are read, other project runners and code that starts programs ask; xargs and parallel are read with their input; kubectl's subcommand is found the way cobra finds it, and a dynamic subcommand or an unknown root flag asks; one escalation-key table serves label, annotate and patch; cluster lifecycle tools ask; the hook's parent accepts only an ask on stdout. Fourth review: the allow set is the shipped contract and is written down in full -- a table in docs/use-cases.md of every shape that allows, asks or denies, each row run by a test, so the docs and the code cannot drift; the review's 223 ordinary commands and its documented asks are pinned as corpora. port-forward and proxy are their own class (connects), allowed unless the port is reachable from other hosts; a dry run on any verb that defines the flag persists nothing and is allowed, with a note when a real run of the manifest would be denied, and the flag earns nothing on exec or a plugin; a command that carries a manifest with an explicit target (--context, --cluster, --kubeconfig, KUBECONFIG=) is not determined because the platform the manifest is judged against is that context's, while a day-2 command with no manifest is judged the same with or without one; --help on any verb, oc adm node-logs, must-gather, release info, the upgrade status, oc registry info, krew list|info|search, neat and helm diff are allowed, and attach, extract, login, logout, registry login, krew install and config use-context ask with their own reason; a command past the reader's 20,000-character cap is allowed when it names no kube tool anywhere and not determined when it does; verify and the hook write under an ASCII encoding instead of exiting 4; the hook's timeout message states the budget it ran with. Fifth review: --dry-run is read with pflag's arity rule (a bare non-bool flag takes the next word, an unknown bare flag fails closed), for helm too, and a run-time word after it asks; past the 20,000-character cap only one inert echo, printf or here-document is allowed; delete --raw, -f or a second kind is not a pod by name, nor is any day-2 change with a run-time word, and the last -p patch is the one judged; --help is allowed on built-in verbs only; --post-renderer and kustomize's --enable-* ask on every verb first; --as, --token, --server, --user and the certificate flags ask on every change; every credential form is redacted from the printed reason; oc adm allows only its read list; PowerShell constructs that build or run a command ask; set env of PATH, proxy and CA variables, expose --type that is not a literal ClusterIP, control-plane namespaces and workloads, create secret docker-registry, neat with a non-read verb, a widened proxy filter and must-gather --image ask; hidden characters are marked <U+XXXX> identically under every encoding; the review's 1,106 lines are pinned in three corpora. G310 A deterministic verdict nobody can re-derive is a claim, not evidence -- and a verifier that cannot get an input must never report a pass. audit --receipt PATH and gate --receipt PATH write one self-describing document binding the schema, the tool version, every input by SHA-256 with the path as given and the path it resolved to, one digest over the whole input set, a meaning version per rule derived from the rule's own declaration rather than hand-maintained, the settings that changed the verdict, the scope read and not read, and the verdict (counts, the score and the reason there is no verdict as Report.undetermined() decided them -- a receipt records the verdict it was given and never re-judges it -- the exit code, which is 1 for an undetermined report whatever --fail-on says, and every finding by Finding.fingerprint()). receipt verify answers identical (0), differs and names the fields (1), or CANNOT BE CHECKED (5) -- for a missing input, a digest that moved, another kube-noob's version, a rule set that no longer means the same thing, a file added to a directory since, standard input, or a run that cannot be replayed from the files the receipt binds. A changed input is a different question, never a changed verdict; a payload that does not match its own digest is refused (4), not checked. The verdict payload is byte-identical for identical inputs and everything that cannot be deterministic lives in a separate envelope, which records no host and no runner identity and says why. SHA-256 only: the document states in its own text that it proves the same inputs give the same verdict and proves nothing about who produced it
